People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came because… well, since my grandson was killed, something in me has gone terribly wrong. My daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, and I know she loves me, and I know I love her—I know that in my head as clearly as I know my own name—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside. It’s like the feeling part of me is just… not there.

She’ll be right next to me, trying to comfort me, and I can see her doing it, I can understand it, but it doesn’t reach me. That’s what frightens me. I should feel comfort, or love, or something soft when she holds my hand, but instead it feels empty inside. I don’t know how else to explain it. Since what happened to my grandson, I just can’t seem to feel those good feelings, even with my own child sitting right there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know if “sad” is the right word. I know that sounds strange, because of course this is the saddest thing that could happen, isn’t it? A child—my grandson—and everything that went with him. But when you ask if I’ve been feeling sad most of the time, I get stuck, because it doesn’t feel like ordinary sadness that I recognize.

It’s more like I’m hollowed out. Sometimes I think I should be crying or feeling that ache people talk about, but instead I’m just sitting there, knowing something terrible has happened and not knowing how to feel it properly. So… I don’t want to say no, because that sounds wrong. But I also can’t honestly say I feel sad in the normal way most of the time. It’s more empty than sad, if that makes any sense.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… no, I haven’t. I don’t even know if “enjoy” is a word that fits anywhere near me right now. It’s not that I try something I used to like and it disappoints me a little—it’s more like there’s no place inside me for pleasure to land.

My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she’s there. I know she loves me. I know I love her—of course I do, she’s my child. But I can’t feel the warmth of it. That’s the part that scares me most. Even that, even her hand in mine, doesn’t bring comfort or softness or any good feeling. It’s like I understand love with my mind, but I can’t feel it in my chest.

So if I can’t even feel that with my own daughter sitting right next to me, then no… I’m not finding pleasure in anything. It all feels flat and far away.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I keep turning the word over in my head, and sometimes I think, “No, I didn’t do this. I didn’t make this happen.” But then the next minute there’s this awful feeling like I should have somehow been able to stop it, even though I can’t tell you how.

It’s not a simple kind of guilt, like I did one thing wrong and I can point to it. It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.

So yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.

It’s more empty than sad, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to enjoy or find pleasure in things you normally like since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… no, I haven’t. I don’t even know if “enjoy” is a word that fits anywhere near me right now. It’s not that I try something I used to like and it disappoints me a little—it’s more like there’s no place inside me for pleasure to land.\n\nMy daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she’s there. I know she loves me. I know I love her—of course I do, she’s my child. But I can’t feel the warmth of it. That’s the part that scares me most. Even that, even her hand in mine, doesn’t bring comfort or softness or any good feeling. It’s like I understand love with my mind, but I can’t feel it in my chest.\n\nSo if I can’t even feel that with my own daughter sitting right next to me, then no… I’m not finding pleasure in anything. It all feels flat and far away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I keep turning the word over in my head, and sometimes I think, “No, I didn’t do this. I didn’t make this happen.” But then the next minute there’s this awful feeling like I should have somehow been able to stop it, even though I can’t tell you how.\n\nIt’s not a simple kind of guilt, like I did one thing wrong and I can point to it. It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.\n\nSo yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I snapped at my daughter yesterday for moving a chair in the kitchen, shouting at her like she had done something terrible, and afterward I sat there ashamed because it was just a chair.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: When the ice maker drops cubes or a car backfires down the block, my whole body jumps so hard I grab the table and my heart keeps pounding long after everyone else has settled down.\n- key: S082\n  name: Depersonalization_Derealization\n  description: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Sometimes my History]\nDoctor: Have you been able to enjoy or find pleasure in things you normally like since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… no, I haven’t. I don’t even know if “enjoy” is a word that fits anywhere near me right now. It’s not that I try something I used to like and it disappoints me a little—it’s more like there’s no place inside me for pleasure to land.\n\nMy daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she’s there. I know she loves me. I know I love her—of course I do, she’s my child. But I can’t feel the warmth of it. That’s the part that scares me most. Even that, even her hand in mine, doesn’t bring comfort or softness or any good feeling. It’s like I understand love with my mind, but I can’t feel it in my chest.\n\nSo if I can’t even feel that with my own daughter sitting right next to me, then no… I’m not finding pleasure in anything. It all feels flat and far away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I keep turning the word over in my head, and sometimes I think, “No, I didn’t do this. I didn’t make this happen.” But then the next minute there’s this awful feeling like I should have somehow been able to stop it, even though I can’t tell you how.\n\nIt’s not a simple kind of guilt, like I did one thing wrong and I can point to it. It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.\n\nSo yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. When you say “not wanting to live,” do you mean have I sat there and thought, “I want to die”? Because I don’t know that I’ve had it as a clear sentence like that.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels so impossible and unreal that I sometimes don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing here, if that makes sense. But I’m afraid to say it wrong, because I don’t want you to think I mean something more specific than I do. I haven’t really sorted it out in my own head.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you used to find pleasurable before your grandson's death that you have attempted to do since then?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’ve tried, yes… or I suppose I should say I’ve put myself near the things I used to find comforting, but I don’t know that I’ve really done them in the same way.

The clearest thing is being with my daughter. Before all of this, just having her sit with me, hold my hand, talk to me quietly—there would have been comfort in that. Real comfort. I would feel it, you know, that warmth of being with my child, that sense that love was right there in the room with us.

And now she can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she loves me. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.

So yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. Since he was killed, even that feels distant and flat.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because… well, since my grandson was killed, something in me has gone terribly wrong. My daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, and I know she loves me, and I know I love her—I know that in my head as clearly as I know my own name—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside. It’s like the feeling part of me is just… not there.\n\nShe’ll be right next to me, trying to comfort me, and I can see her doing it, I can understand it, but it doesn’t reach me. That’s what frightens me. I should feel comfort, or love, or something soft when she holds my hand, but instead it feels empty inside. I don’t know how else to explain it. Since what happened to my grandson, I just can’t seem to feel those good feelings, even with my own child sitting right there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emptiness inside since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been six days now. Since the day he was killed, outside the community center. I keep counting it, even though I don’t want to—six days, and it feels both like it just happened this minute and like I’ve been stuck in it forever. That empty feeling started right after that, and it hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if “sad” is the right word. I know that sounds strange, because of course this is the saddest thing that could happen, isn’t it? A child—my grandson—and everything that went with him. But when you ask if I’ve been feeling sad most of the time, I get stuck, because it doesn’t feel like ordinary sadness that I recognize.\n\nIt’s more like I’m hollowed out. Sometimes I think I should be crying or feeling that ache people talk about, but instead I’m just sitting there, knowing something terrible has happened and not knowing how to feel it properly. So… I don’t want to say no, because that sounds wrong. But I also can’t honestly say I feel sad in the normal way most of the time. It’s more empty than sad, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to enjoy or find pleasure in things you normally like since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… no, I haven’t. I don’t even know if “enjoy” is a word that fits anywhere near me right now. It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. History]\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I keep turning the word over in my head, and sometimes I think, “No, I didn’t do this. I didn’t make this happen.” But then the next minute there’s this awful feeling like I should have somehow been able to stop it, even though I can’t tell you how.\n\nIt’s not a simple kind of guilt, like I did one thing wrong and I can point to it. It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.\n\nSo yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. When you say “not wanting to live,” do you mean have I sat there and thought, “I want to die”? Because I don’t know that I’ve had it as a clear sentence like that.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels so impossible and unreal that I sometimes don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing here, if that makes sense. But I’m afraid to say it wrong, because I don’t want you to think I mean something more specific than I do. I haven’t really sorted it out in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable before your grandson's death that you have attempted to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, yes… or I suppose I should say I’ve put myself near the things I used to find comforting, but I don’t know that I’ve really done them in the same way.\n\nThe clearest thing is being with my daughter. Before all of this, just having her sit with me, hold my hand, talk to me quietly—there would have been comfort in that. Real comfort. I would feel it, you know, that warmth of being with my child, that sense that love was right there in the room with us.\n\nAnd now she can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she loves me. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.\n\nSo yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. Since he was killed, even that feels distant and flat.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead since your grandson passed away?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… “better off dead” is such a frightening way to put it, and I’m not saying that to criticize you. I know you have to ask plainly. It’s just that when I hear those words, I freeze a little, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more definite than what I actually feel.

Since he died, I have had moments where I feel like I don’t understand how life is supposed to keep going. Like everything has been knocked out from under me and I’m still somehow sitting here, breathing and answering questions, but I don’t really know why the world is continuing in the way it is. But that isn’t exactly the same as thinking, “I would be better off dead.” At least, I don’t think it is.

I suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t had it all arranged in my mind like a clear thought or decision. It’s more a fog, a heaviness, and sometimes a feeling that I don’t know where I belong now. But I’m hesitant to say yes to that specific phrase, because I don’t know that I’ve thought it in those words.

minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: When the ice maker drops cubes or a car backfires down the block, my whole body jumps so hard I grab the table and my heart keeps pounding long after everyone else has settled down.\n- key: S082\n  name: Depersonalization_Derealization\n  description: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Sometimes my own living room looks flat and strange, like I'm looking at it through church glass, and I feel as if I'm watching this old woman move around instead of being fully in my own body.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have stopped going to my walking club and I cannot bring myself to help with the funeral repast planning, even though those are the very people and duties my family depends on me for.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 6 days\n  manifestation: This has been going on for the 6 days since my grandson was killed outside the community center.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 84 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S076\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. When you say “not wanting to live,” do you mean have I sat there and thought, “I want to die”? Because I don’t know that I’ve had it as a clear sentence like that.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels so impossible and unreal that I sometimes don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing here, if that makes sense. But I’m afraid to say it wrong, because I don’t want you to think I mean something more specific than I do. I haven’t really sorted it out in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable before your grandson's death that you have attempted to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, yes… or I suppose I should say I’ve put myself near the things I used to find comforting, but I don’t know that I’ve really done them in the same way.\n\nThe clearest thing is being with my daughter. Before all of this, just having her sit with me, hold my hand, talk to me quietly—there would have been comfort in that. Real comfort. I would feel it, you know, that warmth of being with my child, that sense that love was right there in the room with us.\n\nAnd now she can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she loves me. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.\n\nSo yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. Since he was killed, even that feels distant and flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “better off dead” is such a frightening way to put it, and I’m not saying that to criticize you. I know you have to ask plainly. It’s just that when I hear those words, I freeze a little, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more definite than what I actually feel.\n\nSince he died, I have had moments where I feel like I don’t understand how life is supposed to keep going. Like everything has been knocked out from under me and I’m still somehow sitting here, breathing and answering questions, but I don’t really know why the world is continuing in the way it is. But that isn’t exactly the same as thinking, “I would be better off dead.” At least, I don’t think it is.\n\nI suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t had it all arranged in my mind like a clear thought or decision. It’s more a fog, a heaviness, and sometimes a feeling that I don’t know where I belong now. But I’m hesitant to say yes to that specific phrase, because I don’t know that I’ve thought it in those words.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I have, though it feels a little different than just “losing interest,” if that makes sense. It’s not only that I don’t want to do things. It’s that even the things that used to mean the most to me don’t feel the way they used to.

I came in because since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know she loves me. I can look at her and understand that this is my child, and that closeness should bring me comfort. Before, that would have warmed me from the inside, just sitting there with her. But now I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all.

So when you ask about activities I used to enjoy… I don’t know if sitting with my daughter counts as an “activity,” exactly, but it was one of the things that gave me the most peace in the world. And now it’s like I’m present for it, but I can’t reach the feeling. It’s gone flat somehow.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because… well, since my grandson was killed, something in me has gone terribly wrong. My daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, and I know she loves me, and I know I love her—I know that in my head as clearly as I know my own name—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside. It’s like the feeling part of me is just… not there.\n\nShe’ll be right next to me, trying to comfort me, and I can see her doing it, I can understand it, but it doesn’t reach me. That’s what frightens me. I should feel comfort, or love, or something soft when she holds my hand, but instead it feels empty inside. I don’t know how else to explain it. Since what happened to my grandson, I just can’t seem to feel those good feelings, even with my own child sitting right there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emptiness inside since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been six days now. Since the day he was killed, outside the community center. I keep counting it, even though I don’t want to—six days, and it feels both like it just happened this minute and like I’ve been stuck in it forever. That empty feeling started right after that, and it hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if “sad” is the right word. I know that sounds strange, because of course this is the saddest thing that could happen, isn’t it? A child—my grandson—and everything that went with him. But when you ask if I’ve been feeling sad most of the time, I get stuck, because it doesn’t feel like ordinary sadness that I recognize.\n\nIt’s more like I’m hollowed out. Sometimes I think I should be crying or feeling that ache people talk about, but instead I’m just sitting there, knowing something terrible has happened and not knowing how to feel it properly. So… I don’t want to say no, because that sounds wrong. But I also can’t honestly say I feel sad in the normal way most of the time. It’s more empty than sad, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to enjoy or find pleasure in things you normally like since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… no, I haven’t. I don’t even know if “enjoy” is a word that fits anywhere near me right now. It’s not that I try something I used to like and it disappoints me a little—it’s more like there’s no place inside me for pleasure to land.\n\nMy daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she’s there. I know she loves me. I know I love her—of course I do, she’s my child. But I can’t feel the warmth of it. That’s the part that scares me most. Even that, even her hand in mine, doesn’t bring comfort or softness or any good feeling. It’s like I understand love with my mind, but I can’t feel it in my chest.\n\nSo if I can’t even feel that with my own daughter sitting right next to me, then no… I’m not finding pleasure in anything. It all feels flat and far away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I keep turning the word over in my head, and sometimes I think, “No, I didn’t do this. I didn’t make this happen.” But then the next minute there’s this awful feeling like I should have somehow been able to stop it, even though I can’t tell you how.\n\nIt’s not a simple kind of guilt, like I did one thing wrong and I can point to it. It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.\n\nSo yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. When you say “not wanting to live,” do you mean have I sat there and thought, “I want to die”? Because I don’t know that I’ve had it as a clear sentence like that.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels so impossible and unreal that I sometimes don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing here, if that makes sense. But I’m afraid to say it wrong, because I don’t want you to think I mean something more specific than I do. I haven’t really sorted it out in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable before your grandson's death that you have attempted to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, yes… or I suppose I should say I’ve put myself near the things I used to find comforting, but I don’t know that I’ve really done them in the same way.\n\nThe clearest thing is being with my daughter. Before all of this, just having her sit with me, hold my hand, talk to me quietly—there would have been comfort in that. Real comfort. I would feel it, you know, that warmth of being with my child, that sense that love was right there in the room with us.\n\nAnd now she can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she loves me. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.\n\nSo yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. Since he was killed, even that feels distant and flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “better off dead” is such a frightening way to put it, and I’m not saying that to criticize you. I know you have to ask plainly. It’s just that when I hear those words, I freeze a little, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more definite than what I actually feel.\n\nSince he died, I have had moments where I feel like I don’t understand how life is supposed to keep going. Like everything has been knocked out from under me and I’m still somehow sitting here, breathing and answering questions, but I don’t really know why the world is continuing in the way it is. But that isn’t exactly the same as thinking, “I would be better off dead.” At least, I don’t think it is.\n\nI suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t had it all arranged in my mind like a clear thought or decision. It’s more a fog, a heaviness, and sometimes a feeling that I don’t know where I belong now. But I’m hesitant to say yes to that specific phrase, because I don’t know that I’ve thought it in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though it feels a little different than just “losing interest,” if that makes sense. It’s not only that I don’t want to do things. It’s that even the things that used to mean the most to me don’t feel the way they used to.\n\nI came in because since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know she loves me. I can look at her and understand that this is my child, and that closeness should bring me comfort. Before, that would have warmed me from the inside, just sitting there with her. But now I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all.\n\nSo when you ask about activities I used to enjoy… I don’t know if sitting with my daughter counts as an “activity,” exactly, but it was one of the things that gave me the most peace in the world. And now it’s like I’m present for it, but I can’t reach the feeling. It’s gone flat somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to my weight, to be honest. I’m not sure I’ve even thought to check, not in any organized way.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because… well, since my grandson was killed, something in me has gone terribly wrong. My daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, and I know she loves me, and I know I love her—I know that in my head as clearly as I know my own name—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside. It’s like the feeling part of me is just… not there.\n\nShe’ll be right next to me, trying to comfort me, and I can see her doing it, I can understand it, but it doesn’t reach me. That’s what frightens me. I should feel comfort, or love, or something soft when she holds my hand, but instead it feels empty inside. I don’t know how else to explain it. Since what happened to my grandson, I just can’t seem to feel those good feelings, even with my own child sitting right there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emptiness inside since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been six days now. Since the day he was killed, outside the community center. I keep counting it, even though I don’t want to—six days, and it feels both like it just happened this minute and like I’ve been stuck in it forever. That empty feeling started right after that, and it hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if “sad” is the right word. I know that sounds strange, because of course this is the saddest thing that could happen, isn’t it? A child—my grandson—and everything that went with him. But when you ask if I’ve been feeling sad most of the time, I get stuck, because it doesn’t feel like ordinary sadness that I recognize.\n\nIt’s more like I’m hollowed out. Sometimes I think I should be crying or feeling that ache people talk about, but instead I’m just sitting there, knowing something terrible has happened and not knowing how to feel it properly. So… I don’t want to say no, because that sounds wrong. But I also can’t honestly say I feel sad in the normal way most of the time. It’s more empty than sad, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to enjoy or find pleasure in things you normally like since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… no, I haven’t. I don’t even know if “enjoy” is a word that fits anywhere near me right now. It’s not that I try something I used to like and it disappoints me a little—it’s more like there’s no place inside me for pleasure to land.\n\nMy daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she’s there. I know she loves me. I know I love her—of course I do, she’s my child. But I can’t feel the warmth of it. That’s the part that scares me most. Even that, even her hand in mine, doesn’t bring comfort or softness or any good feeling. It’s like I understand love with my mind, but I can’t feel it in my chest.\n\nSo if I can’t even feel that with my own daughter sitting right next to me, then no… I’m not finding pleasure in anything. It all feels flat and far away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I keep turning the word over in my head, and sometimes I think, “No, I didn’t do this. I didn’t make this happen.” But then the next minute there’s this awful feeling like I should have somehow been able to stop it, even though I can’t tell you how.\n\nIt’s not a simple kind of guilt, like I did one thing wrong and I can point to it. It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.\n\nSo yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. When you say “not wanting to live,” do you mean have I sat there and thought, “I want to die”? Because I don’t know that I’ve had it as a clear sentence like that.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels so impossible and unreal that I sometimes don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing here, if that makes sense. I haven’t really sorted it out in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable before your grandson's death that you have attempted to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, yes… or I suppose I should say I’ve put myself near the things I used to find comforting, but I don’t know that I’ve really done them in the same way.\n\nThe clearest thing is being with my daughter. Before all of this, just having her sit with me, hold my hand, talk to me quietly—there would have been comfort in that. Real comfort. I would feel it, you know, that warmth of being with my child, that sense that love was right there in the room with us.\n\nAnd now she can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she loves me. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.\n\nSo yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.\n\nSo yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. When you say “not wanting to live,” do you mean have I sat there and thought, “I want to die”? Because I don’t know that I’ve had it as a clear sentence like that.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels so impossible and unreal that I sometimes don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing here, if that makes sense. But I’m afraid to say it wrong, because I don’t want you to think I mean something more specific than I do. I haven’t really sorted it out in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable before your grandson's death that you have attempted to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, yes… or I suppose I should say I’ve put myself near the things I used to find comforting, but I don’t know that I’ve really done them in the same way.\n\nThe clearest thing is being with my daughter. Before all of this, just having her sit with me, hold my hand, talk to me quietly—there would have been comfort in that. Real comfort. I would feel it, you know, that warmth of being with my child, that sense that love was right there in the room with us.\n\nAnd now she can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she loves me. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.\n\nSo yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. Since he was killed, even that feels distant and flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “better off dead” is such a frightening way to put it, and I’m not saying that to criticize you. I know you have to ask plainly. It’s just that when I hear those words, I freeze a little, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more definite than what I actually feel.\n\nSince he died, I have had moments where I feel like I don’t understand how life is supposed to keep going. Like everything has been knocked out from under me and I’m still somehow sitting here, breathing and answering questions, but I don’t really know why the world is continuing in the way it is. But that isn’t exactly the same as thinking, “I would be better off dead.” At least, I don’t think it is.\n\nI suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t had it all arranged in my mind like a clear thought or decision. It’s more a fog, a heaviness, and sometimes a feeling that I don’t know where I belong now. But I’m hesitant to say yes to that specific phrase, because I don’t know that I’ve thought it in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though it feels a little different than just “losing interest,” if that makes sense. It’s not only that I don’t want to do things. It’s that even the things that used to mean the most to me don’t feel the way they used to.\n\nI came in because since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know she loves me. I can look at her and understand that this is my child, and that closeness should bring me comfort. Before, that would have warmed me from the inside, just sitting there with her. But now I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all.\n\nSo when you ask about activities I used to enjoy… I don’t know if sitting with my daughter counts as an “activity,” exactly, but it was one of the things that gave me the most peace in the world. And now it’s like I’m present for it, but I can’t reach the feeling. It’s gone flat somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to my weight, to be honest. I’m not sure I’ve even thought to check, not in any organized way.\n\nMy clothes… I don’t know, they don’t feel so different that I could say, “yes, I’ve lost” or “yes, I’ve gained.” At my age, weight can shift a bit anyway, and I don’t want to guess and make it sound like something I know for certain. I haven’t noticed a significant change, no. If there has been one, it hasn’t been obvious to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t had a clear thought like, “I am going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean. I haven’t sat there making some sort of plan in my head, or thinking through doing something to myself in a direct way.

But since he passed, my mind has not felt very steady. There are moments where everything feels so unbearable and unreal that I get frightened by my own thoughts, not because I’m deciding to do anything, but because I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I feel overwhelmed and lost, and then I start worrying about what that means.

So… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts of actually harming myself. Not in a definite way. But I don’t want to sound too neat about it either, because inside it has been very dark and confusing.

It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.\n\nSo yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. It’s just that when I hear those words, I freeze a little, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more definite than what I actually feel.\n\nSince he died, I have had moments where I feel like I don’t understand how life is supposed to keep going. Like everything has been knocked out from under me and I’m still somehow sitting here, breathing and answering questions, but I don’t really know why the world is continuing in the way it is. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. I keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to make it sound like every single second is exactly the same, but pleasure is the wrong word now.

The clearest example is my daughter. She can sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know in my mind that I love her. I know she loves me. I know that should comfort me, and before all this, it would have. Just having her close would have brought me some warmth, some peace. But now it’s like the feeling doesn’t come through. I can recognize what it is supposed to mean, but I can’t feel it inside.

So if I can’t even feel that warmth with my own daughter beside me, I don’t know what activity I could honestly say gives me pleasure. I may go through motions, or sit with someone, or be present in the room, but it feels flat. Like I’m there, but the part of me that would take comfort or joy from it is just… not reachable.

It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.\n\nSo yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. When you say “not wanting to live,” do you mean have I sat there and thought, “I want to die”? Because I don’t know that I’ve had it as a clear sentence like that.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels so impossible and unreal that I sometimes don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing here, if that makes sense. But I’m afraid to say it wrong, because I don’t want you to think I mean something more specific than I do. I haven’t really sorted it out in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable before your grandson's death that you have attempted to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, yes… or I suppose I should say I’ve put myself near the things I used to find comforting, but I don’t know that I’ve really done them in the same way.\n\nThe clearest thing is being with my daughter. Before all of this, just having her sit with me, hold my hand, talk to me quietly—there would have been comfort in that. Real comfort. I would feel it, you know, that warmth of being with my child, that sense that love was right there in the room with us.\n\nAnd now she can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she loves me. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.\n\nSo yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. Since he was killed, even that feels distant and flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “better off dead” is such a frightening way to put it, and I’m not saying that to criticize you. I know you have to ask plainly. It’s just that when I hear those words, I freeze a little, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more definite than what I actually feel.\n\nSince he died, I have had moments where I feel like I don’t understand how life is supposed to keep going. Like everything has been knocked out from under me and I’m still somehow sitting here, breathing and answering questions, but I don’t really know why the world is continuing in the way it is. But that isn’t exactly the same as thinking, “I would be better off dead.” At least, I don’t think it is.\n\nI suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t had it all arranged in my mind like a clear thought or decision. It’s more a fog, a heaviness, and sometimes a feeling that I don’t know where I belong now. But I’m hesitant to say yes to that specific phrase, because I don’t know that I’ve thought it in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though it feels a little different than just “losing interest,” if that makes sense. It’s not only that I don’t want to do things. It’s that even the things that used to mean the most to me don’t feel the way they used to.\n\nI came in because since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know she loves me. I can look at her and understand that this is my child, and that closeness should bring me comfort. Before, that would have warmed me from the inside, just sitting there with her. But now I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all.\n\nSo when you ask about activities I used to enjoy… I don’t know if sitting with my daughter counts as an “activity,” exactly, but it was one of the things that gave me the most peace in the world. And now it’s like I’m present for it, but I can’t reach the feeling. It’s gone flat somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to my weight, to be honest. I’m not sure I’ve even thought to check, not in any organized way.\n\nMy clothes… I don’t know, they don’t feel so different that I could say, “yes, I’ve lost” or “yes, I’ve gained.” At my age, weight can shift a bit anyway, and I don’t want to guess and make it sound like something I know for certain. I haven’t noticed a significant change, no. If there has been one, it hasn’t been obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t had a clear thought like, “I am going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean. I haven’t sat there making some sort of plan in my head, or thinking through doing something to myself in a direct way.\n\nBut since he passed, my mind has not felt very steady. There are moments where everything feels so unbearable and unreal that I get frightened by my own thoughts, not because I’m deciding to do anything, but because I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I feel overwhelmed and lost, and then I start worrying about what that means.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts of actually harming myself. Not in a definite way. But I don’t want to sound too neat about it either, because inside it has been very dark and confusing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have found some pleasure in since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to make it sound like every single second is exactly the same, but pleasure is the wrong word now.\n\nThe clearest example is my daughter. She can sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know in my mind that I love her. I know she loves me. I know that should comfort me, and before all this, it would have. Just having her close would have brought me some warmth, some peace. But now it’s like the feeling doesn’t come through. I can recognize what it is supposed to mean, but I can’t feel it inside.\n\nSo if I can’t even feel that warmth with my own daughter beside me, I don’t know what activity I could honestly say gives me pleasure. I may go through motions, or sit with someone, or be present in the room, but it feels flat. Like I’m there, but the part of me that would take comfort or joy from it is just… not reachable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Like I’m there, but the part of me that would take comfort or joy from it is just… not reachable.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself constantly thinking about your grandson or his death, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 84 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"I'll be folding a quilt square or reaching for my tea and all of a sudden I hear those shots again and see him lying there outside the community center like it is happening right in front of me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It comes in even when I’m trying very hard not to let it. I can be doing something ordinary, something with my hands, like folding a quilt square, trying to keep the edges straight and just think about the fabric, and then all of a sudden it is not fabric anymore in my mind. I hear those shots again.

Or I’ll be reaching for my tea, just a simple thing, and before I even know what’s happening, I see him lying there outside the community center. Not like a memory that stays politely in the past, but like it is right in front of me again. It interrupts everything. I can be trying to listen to someone speak, or trying to pay attention to what I’m doing, and then there it is.

I don’t feel like I’m choosing to think about it. I wish I could set it down for even a little while, but it pushes its way in. It’s his face, that place, those sounds… and then I’m just back there, even though I’m sitting in my own home with a quilt square in my lap or a cup of tea in my hand.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because… well, since my grandson was killed, something in me has gone terribly wrong. My daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, and I know she loves me, and I know I love her—I know that in my head as clearly as I know my own name—but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside. It’s like the feeling part of me is just… not there.\n\nShe’ll be right next to me, trying to comfort me, and I can see her doing it, I can understand it, but it doesn’t reach me. That’s what frightens me. I should feel comfort, or love, or something soft when she holds my hand, but instead it feels empty inside. I don’t know how else to explain it. Since what happened to my grandson, I just can’t seem to feel those good feelings, even with my own child sitting right there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emptiness inside since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been six days now. Since the day he was killed, outside the community center. I keep counting it, even though I don’t want to—six days, and it feels both like it just happened this minute and like I’ve been stuck in it forever. That empty feeling started right after that, and it hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if “sad” is the right word. I know that sounds strange, because of course this is the saddest thing that could happen, isn’t it? A child—my grandson—and everything that went with him. But when you ask if I’ve been feeling sad most of the time, I get stuck, because it doesn’t feel like ordinary sadness that I recognize.\n\nIt’s more like I’m hollowed out. Sometimes I think I should be crying or feeling that ache people talk about, but instead I’m just sitting there, knowing something terrible has happened and not knowing how to feel it properly. So… I don’t want to say no, because that sounds wrong. But I also can’t honestly say I feel sad in the normal way most of the time. It’s more empty than sad, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to enjoy or find pleasure in things you normally like since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… no, I haven’t. I don’t even know if “enjoy” is a word that fits anywhere near me right now. It’s not that I try something I used to like and it disappoints me a little—it’s more like there’s no place inside me for pleasure to land.\n\nMy daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she’s there. I know she loves me. I know I love her—of course I do, she’s my child. But I can’t feel the warmth of it. That’s the part that scares me most. Even that, even her hand in mine, doesn’t bring comfort or softness or any good feeling. It’s like I understand love with my mind, but I can’t feel it in my chest.\n\nSo if I can’t even feel that with my own daughter sitting right next to me, then no… I’m not finding pleasure in anything. It all feels flat and far away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I keep turning the word over in my head, and sometimes I think, “No, I didn’t do this. I didn’t make this happen.” But then the next minute there’s this awful feeling like I should have somehow been able to stop it, even though I can’t tell you how.\n\nIt’s not a simple kind of guilt, like I did one thing wrong and I can point to it. It’s more… I keep searching for something I missed, some warning, some reason I should have known. And then I tell myself I’m an old woman, I’m not in charge of the world, I couldn’t have protected him from everything. But that doesn’t quiet it for long.\n\nSo yes and no, I suppose. I don’t know if guilt is the right word, but there’s a terrible “what if” feeling that keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I know how to answer that the right way. When you say “not wanting to live,” do you mean have I sat there and thought, “I want to die”? Because I don’t know that I’ve had it as a clear sentence like that.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels so impossible and unreal that I sometimes don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing here, if that makes sense. But I’m afraid to say it wrong, because I don’t want you to think I mean something more specific than I do. I haven’t really sorted it out in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable before your grandson's death that you have attempted to do since then?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried, yes… or I suppose I should say I’ve put myself near the things I used to find comforting, but I don’t know that I’ve really done them in the same way.\n\nThe clearest thing is being with my daughter. Before all of this, just having her sit with me, hold my hand, talk to me quietly—there would have been comfort in that. Real comfort. I would feel it, you know, that warmth of being with my child, that sense that love was right there in the room with us.\n\nAnd now she can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know she loves me. I know I love her. I know that in my head as plainly as I know my own name. But I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It doesn’t reach me. It’s like I’m watching something that should matter, something that used to fill me up, and there’s just… nothing where the feeling ought to be.\n\nSo yes, I’ve attempted that, if that counts. Being close to my daughter used to be one of the most meaningful, comforting things in my life. Since he was killed, even that feels distant and flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “better off dead” is such a frightening way to put it, and I’m not saying that to criticize you. I know you have to ask plainly. It’s just that when I hear those words, I freeze a little, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more definite than what I actually feel.\n\nSince he died, I have had moments where I feel like I don’t understand how life is supposed to keep going. Like everything has been knocked out from under me and I’m still somehow sitting here, breathing and answering questions, but I don’t really know why the world is continuing in the way it is. But that isn’t exactly the same as thinking, “I would be better off dead.” At least, I don’t think it is.\n\nI suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t had it all arranged in my mind like a clear thought or decision. It’s more a fog, a heaviness, and sometimes a feeling that I don’t know where I belong now. But I’m hesitant to say yes to that specific phrase, because I don’t know that I’ve thought it in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though it feels a little different than just “losing interest,” if that makes sense. It’s not only that I don’t want to do things. It’s that even the things that used to mean the most to me don’t feel the way they used to.\n\nI came in because since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know she loves me. I can look at her and understand that this is my child, and that closeness should bring me comfort. Before, that would have warmed me from the inside, just sitting there with her. But now I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all.\n\nSo when you ask about activities I used to enjoy… I don’t know if sitting with my daughter counts as an “activity,” exactly, but it was one of the things that gave me the most peace in the world. And now it’s like I’m present for it, but I can’t reach the feeling. It’s gone flat somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to my weight, to be honest. I’m not sure I’ve even thought to check, not in any organized way.\n\nMy clothes… I don’t know, they don’t feel so different that I could say, “yes, I’ve lost” or “yes, I’ve gained.” At my age, weight can shift a bit anyway, and I don’t want to guess and make it sound like something I know for certain. I haven’t noticed a significant change, no. If there has been one, it hasn’t been obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t had a clear thought like, “I am going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean. I haven’t sat there making some sort of plan in my head, or thinking through doing something to myself in a direct way.\n\nBut since he passed, my mind has not felt very steady. There are moments where everything feels so unbearable and unreal that I get frightened by my own thoughts, not because I’m deciding to do anything, but because I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I feel overwhelmed and lost, and then I start worrying about what that means.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts of actually harming myself. Not in a definite way. But I don’t want to sound too neat about it either, because inside it has been very dark and confusing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have found some pleasure in since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to make it sound like every single second is exactly the same, but pleasure is the wrong word now.\n\nThe clearest example is my daughter. She can sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know in my mind that I love her. I know she loves me. I know that should comfort me, and before all this, it would have. Just having her close would have brought me some warmth, some peace. But now it’s like the feeling doesn’t come through. I can recognize what it is supposed to mean, but I can’t feel it inside.\n\nSo if I can’t even feel that warmth with my own daughter beside me, I don’t know what activity I could honestly say gives me pleasure. I may go through motions, or sit with someone, or be present in the room, but it feels flat. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I wish I could set it down for even a little while, but it pushes its way in. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think that’s true. Though when you say “lost interest,” it feels a little different from just not wanting to do things. It’s more like the feeling has gone out of them.

I came in because since my grandson was killed, even my daughter can sit right beside me holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know that as plainly as I know my own name. I can look at her and understand that she is trying to comfort me, and that before this happened, having her there would have meant everything to me. But now I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like there’s a wall between what I know and what I can feel.

So the things I used to care about… they’re still there in my mind, I suppose. I haven’t forgotten that they mattered. But I can’t reach the caring the way I used to. Even with my own daughter beside me, it feels muted, almost empty, and that frightens me because I don’t want to be that way.
